Democratic Leaders Fear the New Russia Sanctions Bill Could 'Harm' Americans. Here's Why
2026-09-18

Democratic Leaders Fear the New Russia Sanctions Bill Could 'Harm' Americans. Here's Why

TIME | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries speaks to reporters outside of his office at the U.S. Capitol in Washington on Sept. 17, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The House lawmakers on Wednesday passed legislation paving the way for tough new sanctions on Russia that will target financial institutions and the energy industry, which has largely financed the assault on Ukraine. The legislation, which now sits with President Donald Trump for signing, has been hailed for its harsher measures on Russia, with Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky calling it “an extremely powerful tool.” However, Democratic leaders have expressed grave concern over the broader tariff authority it affords to Trump. The legislation allows Trump to impose tariffs of up to 100% on countries that make “new purchases of crude oil or natural gas” originating from Russia, or any country among the top five importers of Russian oil or gas over the last 12 months. According to the Center for Research on Energy and Clean Air (CREA), the top three purchasers of Russian oil and gas in August were China, India, and Turkey. Deborah Elms, head of trade policy at the Hinrich Foundation, says Trump’s previous ambition to introduce tariffs using any available avenue has ignited the concern among Democrats. “The reason why people are so nervous about this is that the authority is extremely wide, and has been handed to an Administration that has already shown great latitude to follow the spirit of the law,” Elms tells TIME. “Tariffs could be set at 100%. It's really at the discretion of the Executive Branch.” The bipartisan effort, named in honor of the late Republican Sen. Lindsey Graham of South Carolina, who spent the final year of his life building support for the measure, passed with 262 votes to 159. Some 58 Democrats voted with the majority of Republicans in favor of the bill, despite objections from party leadership. House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries led the Democratic opposition to the bill, raising concerns over extending Trump new tariff powers that he said could "give the President an additional tool to visit economic harm on the American people." “Why in the world would this Congress or the People's House give this President unfettered authority to visit more tariffs on the world that will have an adverse economic impact on the American people?” the New York Representative questioned ahead of the vote. After the bill passed, Jeffries told TIME that Trump “cannot be entrusted with new powers to impose tariffs that will further raise costs for American families.” TIME has reached out to the White House for comment. Shortly after his return to office, Trump implemented global tariffs as part of what he called a “Liberation Day.” Although the majority of those were shut down by a Supreme Court ruling in February that deemed them illegal, his general use—and threatened use—of tariffs has continued to fuel international tensions. Tariffs introduced since Trump’s return have already made their mark on American households, experts say. The Yale Budget Lab in late August estimated that household costs due to tariffs “total about $1,100 annually under current law.” The U.S.-Canada has raised fresh concerns over the financial impact on American consumers. Many Americans are already struggling with the cost of living crisis. The Urban Institute in April found that “49% of people in American families don’t have the resources to cover essential expenses to live securely in their community.” Rep. Gregory Meeks of New York, the top Democrat on the Foreign Affairs Committee, has also raised concerns about the potential impact on American families. In the weeks leading up to the Russia sanctions bill, he urged lawmakers to find a “compromise that properly punishes Russia for its illegal war of aggression against the Ukrainian people, while protecting the American people from reckless inflationary tariffs.” In a statement to TIME after the vote, Meeks said he found it "regrettable" that lawmakers chose not to “place more guardrails” on the bill’s tariff authorities. Similar concerns were raised by Rep. Don Beyer of Virginia, who described the bill as “well intentioned, but terribly flawed” in its efforts to hinder Russia’s war effort against Ukraine.  “Trump has abused every tariff authority Congress has delegated to the President,” Beyer told TIME, accusing his colleagues of choosing to “ignore our warnings that he is highly likely to continue that pattern with this bill.” Beyer had delivered a stark warning on the floor ahead of the vote, insisting the bill “would do more harm than good for America and for Ukraine.” “Trump has abused every presidential tariff authority—every single one—that Congress has given, often to target our allies. This bill has a loophole that would allow him to define basically any country as a facilitator of evading Russian sanctions,” he argued. “He could then hit them with tariffs of up to 100%, with no guardrails or oversight, and no expiration. It’s a much stronger tariff power than those he has already abused, and it would be much harder to challenge in court. Giving him this power when he has shown us over and over again what he will do with it would be an incredible mistake.” The legislation’s capacity to economically isolate Russia earned it the bipartisan support needed to get the bill to Trump’s desk. The bill allows for the further introduction of sanctions on persons and financial institutions affiliated with the Russian government, the prohibiting of energy exports and investment into Russia, and blocks the transfer of funds to the Kremlin. The bill also includes the potential to expand sanctions on Iran, and comes amid growing concerns from the U.S. over the relationship between Moscow and Tehran. The U.S. Treasury on Monday announced the latest sanctions targeting the Russian bank VTB over allegations it is involved “in Iranian sanctions evasion.” Democratic Rep. Marcy Kaptur of Ohio, who co-chairs and co-founded the Congressional Ukraine Caucus, is in firm favor of the legislation. ”We're going to pay close attention to the Administration and to see what they're going to do with these new powers that have been given,” she told TIME after the vote. Kaptur previously said that both the sanctions and tariff measures were “the best chance we have to get bipartisan legislation signed into law to hold Russia accountable and achieve peace.” Rep. Steny Hoyer of Maryland also praised the legislation, championing it as an additional way to showcase support for Kyiv. “Today we took a step to redeem our pledge to Ukraine, to support their sovereignty and their democracy,” said Hoyer outside the Capitol. “We will sanction, actively and effectively, Russian criminal behavior.” The legislation extends Trump the authority to impose tariffs on new and prominent importers of Russian oil and gas. The legislation could prompt a new wave of tariffs handed out by the President, experts warn. "The tariffs can be introduced across the board," says Elms. “This is what makes it so alarming.” China and India are two major importers of Russian oil and gas and therefore stand to be the most affected. In August, China was importing roughly 1.25 million barrels of Russian Crude oil daily, according to commodity analysts Kpler via Reuters. India imported record-highs of 2.5 million barrels daily of Russian Crude in June, per Kpler's analysis. According to The European External Action Service (EEAS), Russia’s shadow fleet—largely unregulated oil tankers transiting sanctioned oil—includes between 600 to 1,400 vessels. Exports are crucial for China and India, the leading importers of seaborne oil from Russia. U.S. sanctions have already targeted Russian oil tankers and Moscow’s energy industry in response to the full-scale invasion of Ukraine. Still, China and India have remained steady buyers, but the threat of tariffs could change the attitude of both countries, experts predict. “I think they will be quite concerned on this one,” says Elms. “Presumably because of this [bill], we will see some diversification away from Russian energy by India and China.” While the Supreme Court ruling barring most of Trump’s prior tariffs cited Congressional power to impose tariffs, this argument won’t be applicable if lawmakers attempt to restrict the President’s ability. “In this case, Congress has quite clearly given that authority to the President,” says Elms. Beijing has hit back firmly against the tariff measures included in the bill. “China opposes long-arm jurisdiction and unilateral sanctions that have no basis in international law,” said Foreign Ministry spokesperson Guo Jiakun. The legislation comes at a crucial time for U.S.-China relations, just a week ahead of President Xi Jinping’s visit to Washington. U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent is also set to meet with his Chinese counterpart, Vice Premier He Lifeng, this weekend. The U.S. already has a number of tariffs in place on Chinese goods, including a tariff of up to 12.5% on goods allegedly made with forced labor. The U.S. Trade Representative's last year launched a tariff investigation into China's "apparent failure" to comply with the "Phase One" trade deal signed with Trump in 2020. Meanwhile, India’s Foreign Ministry responded to the new sanctions bill by saying it was “monitoring further developments." The statement said India "remains firmly committed to ensuring energy security for its 1.4 billion people" and "will continue to do so through diversified sourcing." The statement said imports of Russian energy had been discussed with U.S. officials in recent months, and that “implications for not just the bilateral relationship, but also the international energy market have been very clearly articulated by the Indian side.” Trump threatened tariffs on Indian goods last August in response to Delhi’s continued purchasing of Russian oil. “They don’t care how many people in Ukraine are being killed by the Russian war machine,” claimed Trump, days ahead of his meeting with President Vladimir Putin that yielded no concrete results. Similar to China, India also currently faces a tariff on alleged forced labor goods, with the levy landing at 10%. After agreeing on a framework for a bilateral trade agreement in February, Delhi and Washington have continued negotiations since. “India and the U.S. have been supposedly a ‘moment away’ from a bilateral agreement,” says Elms. “I think it is highly unlikely now that India will actually sign that agreement because they are now potentially subject to new tariffs at any time.”
The Atlantic
The Michael Kelly Award from The Atlantic is Awarded to Drew Philp for “There Will Be No Mercy” published in The Atavist Magazine
2026-09-18

The Michael Kelly Award from The Atlantic is Awarded to Drew Philp for “There Will Be No Mercy” published in The Atavist Magazine

The Atlantic | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Drew Philp is the winner of the 23rd annual Michael Kelly Award, given by The Atlantic, for his investigative feature “There Will Be No Mercy,” published by The Atavist Magazine in 2025. Philp’s harrowing exposé of Ethiopia’s civil war serves as the definitive account of the two-year conflict, considered one of the deadliest of this century. The award was announced this morning at The Atlantic Festival in New York City. Philp’s article recounted the conflict in Ethiopia through the lens of a single hospital, suddenly overwhelmed with casualties as government soldiers targeted not only combatants but civilians, including children, in the province of Tigray. Philp followed doctors such as Abraha Gebreegziabher, a pediatrician, who confronted the carnage in the hospital even as he wondered why powerful nations simply stood by. In his acceptance speech today, Philp said: “Across the world, people are watching the West break its promises and squander nearly a century of goodwill. Many, like Dr. Abraha, are beginning to look elsewhere for hope and the rule of law. This is a failure that threatens to topple all of our successes. When we in the West abandon the law and commitment to dignity, when we allow international institutions to be corrupted, when we stand idle as our allies commit atrocities—even genocide—and yes, when we commit atrocities ourselves, we corrupt our own democracy and bomb our own credibility abroad and at home.” The Michael Kelly Award judges praised the clarity and poignancy of Philp’s writing and the depth of his reporting on the suffering of the Tigrayan people at the hands of the Ethiopian government. “There Will Be No Mercy” won a National Magazine Award for reporting and a citation for excellence from the Overseas Press Club of America’s Madeline Dane Ross Award. Philp is an independent journalist. He will be awarded a prize of $25,000. Presented annually by The Atlantic, the Michael Kelly Award honors journalists whose work exemplifies “the fearless pursuit and expression of truth,” which defined Michael Kelly’s own life and career. Kelly was the first journalist killed in the course of the Iraq War, in 2003. He served as editor of The Atlantic and National Journal when both magazines were publications of Atlantic Media, chaired by David G. Bradley. Bradley created the award in Kelly’s honor. Journalists from three other news organizations were recognized as finalists: Elliott Woods, at Texas Monthly, for his two-part account of the worst immigration disaster in American history; Emily Baumgaertner Nunn of The New York Times Magazine, for her feature on sex trafficking in Los Angeles; and Elizabeth Weil, at New York magazine’s The Cut, for her moving stories on sibling sexual abuse and nonverbal autistic children. Five judges selected the winner and the finalists: Ena Alvarado, a writer and former assistant editor at The Atlantic; Charles Green, a former editor of National Journal; Adam Harris, a staff writer at The Atlantic; Cullen Murphy, an editor at large of The Atlantic; and Sarah Topol, now a staff writer at The Atlantic and a past winner of the Michael Kelly Award. More information about Michael Kelly and the award in his honor can be found at theatlantic.com/michael-kelly-award. Press Contacts: Anna Bross and Sammi Sontag | The Atlantic [email protected]
The Atlantic
What the Kennedy Center’s Tax Return Reveals
2026-09-18

What the Kennedy Center’s Tax Return Reveals

The Atlantic | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
For 19 months, Donald Trump has portrayed himself as the Kennedy Center’s savior. He promised to root out “woke” programming at the arts complex. He claimed that past leaders had neglected dangerous conditions in the building—a structure that he now says might have to be “ripped down” if he can’t put his name on it. Throughout his tenure, he and his allies have accused former executives of financial mismanagement and even “fraud” that left the organization struggling. Now there’s a fuller picture of the finances Trump inherited—and of what happened during his first months in charge. A previously unseen federal tax filing captures an institution in transition: It spans the final months of its previous leadership and roughly seven months under Trump, offering a baseline for understanding a financial crisis that has grown more severe over the year that has followed. (The Kennedy Center provided me with the filing because nonprofits are required to do so within 30 days of a request.) On paper, the Kennedy Center had an extraordinary financial year. It reported more than $516 million in total revenue on its 990 form for the fiscal year that began in October 2024 and ended last September—a nearly $210 million increase from the previous year. But that windfall appears to have been largely driven by a summer infusion of federal renovation money, which Trump had asked Congress to include in its One Big Beautiful Bill Act and is now threatening to withhold. If you subtract the $257 million in renovation funds from the revenue, the Kennedy Center appears to have spent nearly $47 million more than it took in that fiscal year, a precipitous drop compared with the funds reported on its previous 990. Notably, the new filing includes a $48 million “bad debt expense,” which is essentially a write-down of previously recorded revenue that an organization no longer expects to collect. In the fiscal year ending in 2024, when the center was led by President Deborah Rutter and a bipartisan board of trustees, it brought in about $40 million more than it spent. (The Kennedy Center and the White House did not immediately respond to questions about the return.) Stripping out the renovation money does not necessarily mean the Kennedy Center ran a $47 million deficit last fiscal year. Two experts I spoke with cautioned that the tax return does not provide enough information to make that calculation cleanly. One of them, Michael Kaiser, an arts-management consultant and a former Kennedy Center president, told me that 990s combine operating revenue and expenses with capital funding and other sources of income, making it difficult to isolate the institution’s underlying financial performance from top-line figures. “It combines everything into one mush,” he said. The other expert, Cleopatra Charles, a Rutgers University professor whose research focuses on nonprofit organizations and financial management, reached a similar conclusion. Taken at face value, she told me, the filing depicts a “very financially healthy organization.” Total revenue increased by roughly 68 percent, and contributions and grants more than doubled, allowing the center to report that $210 million surplus. But much of that apparent improvement came from a one-off appropriation rather than the Kennedy Center’s own programming or fundraising efforts. The form noted $301.5 million in federal appropriations—a figure that includes both the money from the One Big Beautiful Bill Act and the roughly $45 million in annual funding the center typically receives. The tax filing bolsters what reporting (and rows of empty seats at performances) over the past year and a half has shown. Program-service revenue, a category that includes ticket sales and other income generated by programming, fell from nearly $105 million the previous year to about $89 million. Kaiser cautioned that this figure can fluctuate considerably depending on the mix of productions. A blockbuster musical such as Hamilton, for instance, could bring a significant bump in ticket revenue that disappears the following year. Other figures on the return raise more questions than answers. There was an unusual increase in net income from unrelated business activities, which rose from about $27,000 in 2023–24 to more than $517,000 in the latest year reported. But the filing does not make clear what generated this increase. Charles pointed out a roughly $43 million increase in “other expenses” that lacked a clear explanation of what they were—including nearly $35 million categorized as “other contracted services.” Taken together, she said, the line items lacked sufficient context about the center’s finances—and an independent audit would offer more clarity. But according to the 990 form, the annual audit was not completed by the time of filing. That’s “very unusual,” a former official with knowledge of Kennedy Center governance and financial practices told me, speaking on the condition of anonymity out of fear of professional repercussions. Audits are usually made available to the public by January or February each year. The Kennedy Center that Trump took over was still recovering from the pandemic, during which it shut down entirely; it did not reopen fully until fall 2021. The center posted deficits during the pandemic but had surpluses in the two years before the Trump takeover. It draws revenue from ticket sales, charitable giving, government funding, campus rentals, and other sources. Trump’s putsch disrupted several of those streams: Many artists canceled performances, a segment of the audience began to boycott the center, and some donors pulled back on contributions. That included perhaps the center’s most important benefactor in recent years. To become the Kennedy Center’s chairman, Trump ousted the private-equity billionaire David Rubenstein, who had donated more than $100 million to the organization. By last fall, ticket sales to classical-music and theater performances were in free fall and the center had taken on a more political flavor, hosting evangelical-Christian and conservative events as well as the World Cup draw, where Trump received the FIFA Peace Prize. But it’s unclear if the Kennedy Center was able to make up the ground it had lost in ticket sales by attracting more donors. The Washington Post reported last month that ticket sales and donations had plunged even further after last December, when a board largely composed of Trump allies voted to rename the Kennedy Center to honor the current president. The ongoing legal battle over the future of the organization centers on two issues: the appropriateness of that board’s decision this March to shut down for the renovation, and whether Trump’s name can be added to a “living memorial” to a slain president. Center officials recently warned that the organization was nearing bankruptcy—confirming many observers’ suspicions. The board wants Trump’s help to raise the necessary funds, and to recognize his efforts by inscribing his name on the building, which a federal judge has rejected several times, saying Congress did not permit such a change when it established the venue. The latest tax document arrives during an extraordinary week at the center. On Tuesday, the judge ruled—again—that the Kennedy Center cannot add Trump’s name. The same day, the board voted—again—for a full closure of the main building, a move that’s still being held up in court. At that meeting, the president upbraided the member of Congress who’s behind the legal fight to halt his efforts, and then went on Truth Social with an ultimatum: He would let a renovation proceed only if federal courts let the board inscribe his name on the building, potentially casting the entire arts complex into operational limbo. Still, even as Trump issued the threat, he also announced he would move $17 million he had raised into a Kennedy Center account to keep the institution afloat. The following day, the center barred the public from entering the building and directed all nonessential personnel to work from home, citing a recent collapse of a portion of the ceiling and other maintenance issues. Trump told reporters that day that in order for the center to be rescued, “I think that the Trump administration should be recognized.” That night, Trump was photographed on Air Force One holding a placard that appeared to read Kennedy Center DEMOLISHED, fueling speculation that a teardown, once seen as far-fetched, has been on his mind all along. Yesterday, the same federal judge issued another order barring the Kennedy Center’s demolition. The financial consequences of those twists and turns won’t come into full view for a while. The latest tax return captures only seven months of Trump’s takeover. It was signed by the Kennedy Center’s chief financial officer, Donna Arduin Kauranen, one of the officials brought in after the transition. Kauranen had previously served as a budget director for several Republican governors, but like many of the new hires under Trump, she hadn’t worked in nonprofit arts management. Even seasoned arts managers would hardly have been prepared for the operational roller coaster that has been Trump’s run at the Kennedy Center. On Tuesday, the same day that Trump made his latest demand, Kauranen resigned.
The Atlantic
The Rise of Parasite Authors
2026-09-18

The Rise of Parasite Authors

The Atlantic | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Editor’s note: This work is part of AI Watchdog, The Atlantic’s ongoing investigation into the generative-AI industry. Earlier this summer, Tamra Westberry received a Facebook notification that made her heart sink. A fellow author had taken to social media to endorse Westberry’s latest book, Project Erebus, after reading about it in a weekly roundup of the latest paranormal-romance releases. Independent authors like Westberry, who is based in Texas and has published dozens of books under the pen name Tara West, usually welcome an encouraging word and a bit of cross-promotion. But Westberry was dismayed. She had never heard of a book called Project Erebus. And she certainly wasn’t its author. She looked up her author profile on Amazon, and there it was: Project Erebus, a book that featured character names she’d previously used, with a cover echoing that of her recent work. Translations were available in German, French, Italian, and Spanish. The book was a complete mystery to her, but after she read its sample pages, she suspected it was made by someone who’d used AI tools. (I ran the book’s cover and promotional blurb through Pangram, which suggested that they were completely AI-generated.) Westberry emailed Amazon and requested that the company delink the unaffiliated books from her profile. Amazon removed the books from the platform within a week or so—but not before some of her fans purchased them, read them, and left reviews. “No real detail, characters remain undeveloped. I almost felt I was reading an essay written by a school child,” one disappointed reader wrote in a two-star review for Project Erebus on Goodreads, a literary social network owned by Amazon that had also attributed the book to West. Read: These 183,000 Books Are Fueling The Biggest Fight In Publishing And Tech Across categories—fantasy, horror, even coloring books—writers say they’re seeing AI-generated listings crop up on their Amazon author pages. Some of these works are in entirely different genres from their usual work—such as a children’s series misattributed to a writer of steamy love stories—whereas others echo the subject matter or jacket designs of preexisting books. One writer reported someone sneaking in a fake edition under the listing of their real book. Because Amazon often sends out emails notifying author-page followers about new listings, these titles are able to leech a writer’s fan base, make ill-gotten revenue, and confuse readers. AI companies pirated millions of books to train today’s most popular models, as The Atlantic has previously reported. Those models are now being used to churn out books in a matter of minutes and place them alongside books that represent years of hard work. In some cases, people go beyond mass-producing second-rate content or publishing copycats of new releases, using the names of established authors on parasitic works that feed off preexisting brands. Writers feel that they have little recourse regarding what AI models ingest, or spit out. “It’s deeply unsettling, after I spent years crafting stories and building my brand, that some no-talent grifter can bottom-feed off my fan base with AI slop,” Westberry, who’s written more than 50 books that were included in a data set used by the likes of OpenAI, Meta, and Anthropic, told me. The parasite-creator problem is pervasive. Professors have found AI-generated academic papers published under their names; recent reporting suggests that sneaking AI-generated songs onto artists’ streaming profiles is a relatively simple move. The issue has also become endemic to Amazon, the world’s largest online book retailer, which sells hundreds of millions of books annually in the U.S. alone. The platform groups works with the same byline under a unified profile thanks to a largely automatic system. Authors say that Amazon has failed to make a concerted effort to address the phenomenon, and that the onus falls on them to monitor their pages, in a crazy-making game of whack-a-mole. An Amazon spokesperson told me that the company does take measures to prevent, detect, and remove content that violates its guidelines, but it did not provide more details about how these systems work. “We are committed to a bookstore that readers love and publishers and authors trust, and work directly with authors to address any issues or errors affecting their accounts,” the spokesperson said. To some degree, Amazon’s parasite-author problem predates the widespread advent of AI. A decade ago, Stephen King publicly urged the site to stop associating his own works with those of Stephen R. King., another horror author whose name was confusing fans. Such instances, however, were few and far between because of the time and effort it required to publish a title. Now that anyone can use LLMs to churn out a novel, the issue has both grown and mutated. While the sheer number of new books published on the platform might inevitably lead to more authors of the same name, Orna Ross, the founder of the Alliance of Independent Authors, noted that many titles are purposefully designed to blend in with a real author’s catalog. “It needs only to fool the algorithm and a rushed buyer for long enough to skim the sale,” she told me. Authors told me that Amazon is typically quick to delink books from author pages once they’ve been flagged. But getting them removed from the platform entirely can be trickier, especially if a writer hasn’t trademarked their name. Amazon can treat such incidents as a matter of “name coincidence,” Ross said, and leave books up even after decoupling them from profiles. Writers struggling to receive direct action from Amazon have also escalated cases through the Authors Guild, the largest professional organization for writers in America, whose recent reports have warned of AI books falsely attributed to writers. For many writers of genre fiction, which relies on high output and familiar tropes, staying afloat means playing a volume game, releasing numerous books a year to make a living. That makes certain genres particularly susceptible to AI dupes, which can blend in with the thousands of real titles released annually. Because producing AI versions is so cheap, even a few sales can be profitable. “The people that are making money selling AI copies are generally doing so by selling one or two or three copies of the thousand different books that they publish,” Julie Trelstad, a publishing consultant, told me. The identity of the individuals behind these books is a mystery to real authors, and often difficult to track down. Regardless, authors don’t view such books as a major sales threat, given that they tend to be less appealing to buyers than genuine works, with slapdash covers and lackluster page counts. What writers are really concerned about is the potential for long-lasting reputational harm among readers, who typically trust that a book listed on an Amazon author page was, indeed, written by said author. Last year, Vanessa O’Loughlin, an Irish author who writes crime books under the name Sam Blake, was alerted to a trilogy of “dark romantasy” books on her profile that she suspected were made with AI. She didn’t write them, for one, and the pages she read felt off. The books revolved around a character named Elara Vance, a combination of popular first and last names used by AI models. O’Loughlin managed to get the stories delinked from her profile within a few days, although they remained online with Sam Blake listed as the author for some weeks after, and sold a few copies. “Dang it! I got fooled by this,” one fan wrote after O’Loughlin warned followers about the books on social media. O’Loughlin fears that new readers who accidentally came across the trilogy might never pick up a Sam Blake book again. In some cases, parasite titles can even latch on to real ones. This past July, Noelle W. Ihli published The Last to Drown, a successful thriller that’s listed at $16 for a paperback on Amazon. But earlier this month, the book’s Amazon page also gave viewers the option to purchase another paperback version that featured the same title and a nearly indistinguishable cover. This edition included a slightly different summary that is 100 percent AI-generated, according to Pangram, and its interior pages appeared to be AI-written as well. It also sold for $5 more. Ihli discovered the title after being alerted by confused readers who had received a suspicious copy in the mail, and it was removed earlier this week, shortly after she filed an intellectual-property-infringement claim with Amazon. “Unless I’m checking obsessively every day, it’s really easy for something like this to slip through the cracks,” Ihli told me. Amazon has introduced some safeguards in response to the onslaught of AI-generated listings. The company restricts the number of books an author can self-publish to 10 a week per book format, and sometimes requests identity verification for accounts. Even so, authors continue to run into variations of the same issue. A romance author who writes under the name Olivia Sinclair (and who asked to keep her legal name private for work and family reasons) previously got Amazon to remove a number of suspected AI books from her profile and thought the problem had been solved until six children’s books about the adventures of cartoon turtles and foxes were listed on her page in July and August. The books, all of which contained summaries flagged as 100 percent AI-written by Pangram, were delinked from her profile earlier this month, shortly after I notified Amazon of the listings. They still remain online with Sinclair listed as the author, however, in addition to at least two seemingly duplicate copies with yet another author name. The process of removing these works appears to be primarily complaint-driven: When Amazon does act, it does so reactively, instead of preemptively, according to Ross, the Alliance of Independent Authors’ founder. “Verified authorship at upload would end most of this overnight,” she said. Amazon did not provide a response when asked why it doesn’t verify profile identities across the board. Meanwhile, authors feel largely powerless. For self-published writers in particular, selling books on Amazon is a key aspect of their livelihood. “It’s such a catch-22,” Anna Blakely, a writer who has also run into seemingly AI-generated books on her profile. (Blakely is a pen name; I agreed to withhold the author's real name to protect her privacy.) She predicts that roughly 90 percent of her income comes from sales on the platform. “There’s really no other viable option.” Other platforms claim to be taking a proactive approach to tackling their own versions of parasite authors. Last year, Spotify announced plans to crack down on songs—AI-generated or otherwise—erroneously appearing on artists’ profiles. The company said it would invest more in its response process for content mismatches, in addition to introducing a new impersonation policy and more recently unveiling a feature for artists to review new music on their profiles. But even these efforts appear to be ineffective: A recent 404 Media report demonstrated how AI-generated songs can still be easily uploaded to real artists’ profiles on streaming services including Spotify and Amazon Music. Keeping an eye on this flood takes up valuable time and effort that authors could have otherwise put toward writing real books. It also adds insult to injury for writers whose books helped refine the AI tools being used to rip off their work. “It’s a devaluing of words, a devaluing of ideas, a devaluing of the accomplishment and the excitement of literature,” said Ben H. Winters, another author who has found what appears to be AI-generated work released under his name on Amazon. Like the rest of the writers I spoke with, his work surfaced in The Atlantic’s searchable database of copyrighted content that trained AI models. “They’ve assimilated all of this stuff, and now they’re spitting it back at us.”
The Atlantic
The Goal of Contemporary Journalism
2026-09-18

The Goal of Contemporary Journalism

The Atlantic | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
In a conversation at The Atlantic Festival, Jeffrey Goldberg, The Atlantic’s editor in chief, spoke with Amna Nawaz, a co-anchor and co-managing editor of PBS NewsHour, and Joe Kahn, the executive editor of The New York Times, about the state of independent media. In his second term, Donald Trump has escalated his confrontations with the press. The New York Times was personally sued by Trump for $15 billion and, separately, the paper’s journalists were recently served subpoenas for reporting on the Qatari jet gifted to the White House. (The Department of Justice subsequently withdrew these subpoenas.) Federal support for PBS was scrapped last year. And as self-styled journalists proliferate online, public trust in the media is at record lows. Evan Smith, the panel’s moderator and The Atlantic’s managing director for events, referred to the guests as outliers for having weathered these attacks—“You’ve survived.” Nawaz responded, “I wouldn’t put it in the past tense. We are surviving.” With so many “content creators” and pundits now claiming to be journalists, Goldberg reflected on how the internet has changed the journalism industry. “There was this view for a while—in the last 20 years—that the democratization of media was an unadulterated good thing,” he noted. Indeed, for decades, the media was “too stringent about who gets to talk.” But with that shift, “standards were lowered. Standards for what counts as actual verified information.” Public distrust in the media, and the president’s frequent attacks on reporters and their employers, have made the work of journalism more difficult. “I remember, back in 2017, boarding a plane with my then-4-year-old daughter, and the man ahead of us was wearing a T-shirt that said in block letters on the back: Journalist. Tree. Rope. Some assembly required,” Nawaz said. To restore public faith, the panelists agreed that journalists need to be transparent about the work that they do. “The further that news and information travels away from the individual human-being journalists who do the work, the less trust there is,” Kahn said. “Original reporting is still by far the most valuable thing.” He later added: “Kind of objectively, I think we’re killing it.” The following conversation has been condensed and edited for clarity. Evan Smith: What is the job of journalism today? Jeff, we talked about this when you were on my PBS show a few months ago. Here’s what you said: “The goal of any quality journalism operation these days is to put as much truth into the universe as possible.” Say more about that, please. Jeffrey Goldberg: I continue to agree with what I said to you on your show. I endorse my statement of six months ago. There’s two levels of your question, “What do editors in chief do?,” and mostly what we do is keep things moving. Tinker. Try to go deep on the problems that exist day to day. The last 11 years have helped us focus on core tasks. We thought most questions were settled: The First Amendment is the First Amendment; the freedom of the press is vitally important; the media plays an indispensable role in any democracy. These things have come under pressure. That’s bad for civilization, that they’re under pressure and that these questions are unsettled. It’s good for us in the sense that it helps clarify the purpose. So you wake up every morning with even more of a sense of mission than maybe you would in ordinary times. I am not saying that we are politicized. I always tell our staff this, and I’m borrowing from Marty Baron, the former editor of The Washington Post: We’re not going to war. We’re not part of the resistance. We’re just going to work. And as long as you just remember that your job every day is to find out something that’s true, contextualize it, treat it fairly, and write it accurately, that’s great. You finished the day by putting something out there. You hope that people are taking to it. You hope that it elevates the ideology of truth. But that’s what you do. It’s sad that these concepts are under pressure. I can only speak for myself, but it’s great to wake up with that purpose. Smith: Joe, I would agree with Jeff. This is an extraordinary moment. Truth isn’t truth. Feelings are facts. Reality is subjective. Conspiracy theories and misinformation are running rampant. It is harder than ever to meet the Goldberg standard, isn’t it? Joe Kahn: It is. And the task for us is to do, as Jeffrey said, to wake up every day, to do original reporting. Ideally, bring new information to light on all the stories that matter every day, and to get it out there, increasingly in forms that people want to consume that information. And that mission is under stress, but it’s more critical than ever. And it makes the job even more vital to American democracy than it ever was. And I would really emphasize the original reporting and new information. We’re in an environment where people feel like they can get information in their feeds nonstop, 24 hours a day. It’s buzzing at them on their phones. They’re hearing it in their AirPods, but actual new original reporting that relies on expert reporters who are going to the scene of news, who are asking tough questions, is in short supply, and it’s on us to find a way to get that to more people. Smith: Amna, the stakes could not be higher in this extraordinary moment. Or, at least, that’s what we all keep saying over and over. Informed communities strengthen democracy. Uninformed communities weaken democracy. It’s a little grandiose-sounding. But speaking of truth, that is true. Those are the stakes. Amna Nawaz: Information is power. That is a basic fact. And in a democracy, an informed electorate is what powers our democracy. That’s also a basic fact. I couldn’t agree more with everything that Joe and Jeff just said. I look at it as the advice I got from my very first boss in journalism. He told me that to be a good journalist is a lot like just being a good person in life: Most of it is showing up. You have to show up. And that means showing up on the ground. It means going to cover the stories that not everybody is covering. It means reporting from the front lines. It also means showing up when you’re able to have access to someone in a position of power to hold them accountable. The way I think about my job every single day is: I’m not asking questions that I necessarily want the answers to. I’m asking questions on behalf of everyone out there who can’t be in that room, who can’t put questions to that person in power. The threats that we’re up against now, they’re not like anything we faced in recent times. I compare it to some of the stuff that I’ve seen covering other countries, other autocratic, anti-democratic leaders in other nations. The same hallmarks are happening here now. It’s a worrying time for journalists, but the best we can do every day is show up and, as I like to say, just put something good out into the world every day. Paul Farhi: Trump’s campaign to crush the media Smith: Jeff, the job of journalism, isn’t it also to set an agenda of sorts to tee up the things for people that they should stop and pay attention to in their busy lives? The curatorial piece of this, the choices we make, that is also part of our job. Goldberg: There was this view for a while, in the last 20 years, that the democratization of media was an unadulterated good thing. Obviously, the First Amendment allows anyone with a piece of paper and a pencil to become a journalist. That’s great. And what happened was the barriers to entry lowered, obviously because of the internet, and everybody thought that was a good thing. But standards were lowered. Standards for what counts as actual verified information. It actually—believe it or not—takes training and practice and more practice and more practice to get it right as a reporter. You can’t just jump into this on the deep end. We were having a very polite dinner party in the media probably until 20, 25 years ago. And then, somebody described to me, the barbarians put ladders up and came up and started squatting on the dining-room table. And this person, obviously an older journalist, thought this was a straight-up bad idea. We probably, as an institution, were too stringent about who gets to talk and who doesn’t get to talk, or who doesn’t have their voices amplified. But now, it is a chaos of misinformation and bad motivation. I’m not saying we’re perfect. God knows. Right? But we’ve got to go back to finding a balance, and we have to explain to people how we do what we do and why we do what we do. Show the work. We have succeeded on any day in which we find out something new that is true and tell that truth, despite the fact that other powerful people don’t want us to tell it. I would just add something to what Joe said. We had Maggie Haberman here yesterday, and John Swan. And Maggie gets a certain amount of criticism, as any famous reporter gets, but something Joe said just kind of triggered this in me. It’s like, half of the things we know about what the Trump administration does is because Maggie has told us. Seriously. I’ll line up a million bloggers and Twitch streamers or whatever and put them against one Maggie. And I know that Maggie’s delivered more right than they’ve ever delivered. They deliver attitude. But Maggie’s got facts. Kahn: Original reporting is still by far the most valuable thing. And it still breaks through——real knowledge and sources and doggedness on the beat and the ability to kind of cut through the spin. People ask, WWhat’s going on with trust in the media? What even is “the media”?? The media is now thousands of influencers online. It’s opinion journalists. It’s self-appointed arbiters of information of all kinds. A tiny, tiny fraction of them actually do original reporting. And that’s what matters more than anything else. And it does break through. Because it’s verified information. It’s factual. It matters. It’s important. And people like Maggie have a track record of getting it right, cycle after cycle, story after story. It matters more than ever. And it does break through. Nawaz: Can I just add to that too? I think when we talk about the way the content moves these days, the fact that our original journalism is out there competing in the same messy and noisy landscape as millions of other content creators, you know, doing TikTok dances in their kitchens. But at the same time, I have this conversation with some very powerful podcasters recently who are not and would not identify themselves as journalists. They rely on our work. They use our original journalism to fuel their conversations and to have something that they can rely on to talk about to their hundreds of millions of followers. And so I think that’s not necessarily a bad thing. There’s an amplification to the work because it gets out in various forms. Nawaz: They are not the same thing. And I think most of them would say that they are not journalists. Smith: Joe, let me ask you, in the context of The New York Times specifically——because The New York Times is one of one,, as opposed to one of many,, as a news source for so many people around this country and around the world—d—do you think, as it relates to setting an agenda, you are meeting the moment? Are you making the right choices, and are you framing your journalism out to the world in a way that the agenda is being set the way it should be said? Kahn: Kind of objectively, I think we’re killing it. Smith: That may be the only quote from this panel. Kahn: The big battle, in my view, is both to keep the best reporters on the most sensitive subjects at a time when they’re absolutely, relentlessly under attack. Motivated and protected by the full force of our security personnel—physical and cybersecurity—and legal support when they’re almost inevitably going to come under legal pressure for doing the kind of reporting that matters. And then also simultaneously finding a way to get it out there in people’s feeds in a way that they want to consume it, and not waiting and relying for them to come to us. And we’re doing both of those things at scale at the moment. We have a long way to go, but I’m really proud of the work that we’ve been able to do in this environment. And by the way, I think we’re winning, honestly. We’re under a lot of pressure,, and I mean, all of us,, wwe’re past the point where people were worried about whether good--quality free press can stand up in this moment in America and still show its resolve, provide good original reporting, withstand the pressure. We’re doing it. And that particular pillar of democracy is strong. Smith: Well, certainly at the national level. We’ll come to local in a little bit. I’m not sure that it’s exactly the same in the middle of the country. I want to ask, though, Jeff, about the people on the receiving end of this. Our readers and our viewers in communities around the country, are they paying attention? Is this a conversation, back to the dinner--party idea, that only we’re having? Or are they actually in the conversation as well about the importance of a free press and the state of journalism? Do they share the view that a free press is important? Do they get that the work we do matters? Do they even understand what that work is? That’s a question I’ve got. Goldberg: It’s an interesting question because,, again, I would just echo what Joe said, the media is a useless term for the type of media we’re talking about. Sometimes, we tend to believe that we do our work and the work is self-explanatory. And everybody has the same civic understanding of the importance of the press. The kind of Jeffersonian understanding: Better to have a free press than a government, even. It’s not true. Can I tell you a very quick story? Goldberg: It’s The Atlantic Festival. I can do it. I’m allowed. I paid for this microphone! Goldberg: This is a really revelatory moment for me, and it changed my understanding of what we have to do to explain the importance of our work and how we do our work. So, in 2020, six years ago, I wrote a story about Donald Trump’s attitude toward veterans and the military. The shorthand of it became “suckers” and “losers.” That he had called WWI veterans buried in a cemetery that he didn’t want to visit because it was raining: He said they’re “suckers” and “losers.” The story was based on my reporting from four anonymous sources. I cited four anonymous sources in the story. Subsequently, by the way, John Kelly, the former White House chief of staff, Marine general, came out and publicly validated it. So, I can say that, but at the time, it was an anonymously sourced story. The White House did what the White House does, right? This was right before the election in 2020. A lot of pressure, a lot of heat, a lot of attacks. I was speaking a couple of months after the story came out, to a group, and someone gets up, an intelligent person, and says something I don’t understand: “Why would you publish a story based on information given to you by people you don’t even know?” And I didn’t understand the question, and I said, “What do you mean?” He says, “Well, you said you had four anonymous sources, but you don’t even know who they are.” (Laughter.) My instinct was to laugh, too, but like, it’s not his fault. And I said “No, no, they’re not anonymous to me. I know them. I know them.” Then I explained the methodology of the information gathering and how we use fact-checking and verified it. And at first I was like, “Oh, wow, that guy doesn’t know much.” And then I thought, “Wait, if people don’t understand what I’m doing, that’s on me as much as it is on them.” And by the way, we’re going to feel this more and more. This is already happening in Europe, where nefarious forces are arguing that what we would think of as probing sources—knocking on doors, calling people up repeatedly, trying to get them to talk to us—this is becoming redefined as harassment, not reporting in a democracy. So we’re going to have to do a much better job of explaining sources and methods and the importance of it. I go back to this Maggie point. You’re criticizing The New York Times for X, Y, and Z. But the only reason you know about X, Y, and Z is because of The New York Times. Smith: Amna, you mentioned threats. I want to talk to the three of you about the threats in the world right now. They come from people in power and they take many forms. They’re financial. They’re legal. They’re political. And, amazingly, they’re physical. It’s not lost on me that the three institutions represented on this stage are exhibits A, B, and C of the new normal. The Atlantic is the subject at the moment of a defamation suit by the FBI director. We stand by our reporting. We’re vigorously defending against that suit. Earlier this year, subpoenas were delivered to the homes of New York Times reporters after reporting on the new Qatari-gifted Air Force One. Subsequently, the Department of Justice “voluntarily” withdrew those subpoenas. Voluntarily in big, fluffy air quotes. PBS has been defunded, and yet you’re still standing here. Smith: But I also need to say that you’re the outliers, in the sense that you’ve survived what I’ve just described. A lot of news organizations don’t have the resources or the stomach to stand up for themselves. They’re either folding in advance, anticipatory obedience, or after the fact. We live in capitulation nation as it relates to these threats. A lot of knee-bending going on, a lot of settlements being paid, a lot of favor being curried. Joe, when you look out at our industry, do you think we’re showing our best selves in response to some of these threats? Nawaz: Can I just say, I’m speaking only on behalf of PBS: Yeah, I think we are. And here’s why. One of the reasons is, as you mentioned, we’ve been defunded. The president made it very clear from the get that he wanted to defund all public media. And we, as part of our mission, remain free for anyone to access all of our content any of the time. So nothing goes behind a paywall. You don’t subscribe to see our journalism. I’m not sure I share Joe’s optimism that we are winning, I will say, and you use the word you’ve survived, I wouldn’t put it in the past tense. We are surviving. But I think we are very much in it. And I don’t think we know how this ends up until we get through it, whatever that looks like. The only way out, to underscore the point you made, is through. And that’s what we have to do every day. It is the journalism that we do every day. It’s the 330 local member stations fighting for their own survival out there on the PBS network. But I am not as optimistic because I’ve seen what’s happened in other countries. We talk about threats and we say it kind of vaguely as if it’s become normal because it has. It is a feature, it is not a bug, of the current political climate. And you go all the way back to a president who ran and called journalists “scum” and “enemies of the people.” I remember back in 2017, boarding a plane with my then-4-year-old daughter and the man ahead of us wearing a T-shirt that said in block letters on the back: Journalist. Tree. Rope. Some assembly required. And me praying that my 4-year-old would not start to sound out the words, as she was doing at the time. That is not normal and it has become normal, and we’re seeing it show up in polling with people increasingly saying things like, Yeah, maybe the government should be able to say when journalists can’t report on something or should have to take a story down because it risks national security. We are in a new normal for this nation. Smith: Yeah. I mean, Joe, I’m going to take Amna’s words here seriously. It does not do justice to the severity or the significance of these threats to say they’re mere shots across the bow. This is a sea change. Kahn: I agree with that. I think just the price of defending against these lawsuits that Trump is throwing around with his very large and essentially full-time legal staff, suing everybody who writes an article that puts him on the defensive in some way. We have a $15 billion lawsuit from Trump. It’s a full-time activity for some of our legal staff, for outside legal counsel. These things cost millions of dollars to defend. The New York Times is in a pretty strong position, so we can defend them. But if you’re another type of organization and you don’t have those resources, then the threat of a lawsuit or the existence of a lawsuit is itself a deterrent. Kahn: It does change it, and it does have an effect. So I acknowledge that. When I’m saying that I think we’re winning, I think if you still step back and look at the overall information environment in this country, it still is vigorous. It still wants to be free. There is a huge amount of debate unfolding in the public space. There’s still really good original reporting being done by quality journalists, both locally and nationally every day, and it is not going to be suppressed. That’s what I mean when we’re winning: It won’t be suppressed. That doesn’t mean that the cost of defending it doesn’t go up. But I’m really confident in the essential, kind of, American essence that this is a country that wants free speech, that’s going to protect free speech. And Donald Trump or any other president isn’t going to shut that down. Smith: Amna, I want to come back to you because I’m personally obsessed with one aspect of these threats, in particular. Women journalists are a special target in this moment. I’m going to say their names. Rachel Scott of ABC the other day, Kristen Holmes of CNN, Catherine Lucey from Bloomberg, to whom the president said “Quiet, piggy.” Kristen Welker threatened the other day with sanctions by the FCC for merely saying on the air that the president’s endorsements had not gone super well. Maggie Haberman, always. All for doing their jobs. We seem to have almost legitimized or normalized that in our response, seems like. I can’t understand why there’s not more outrage in the press corps every time one of these women who asks a question of the president is treated the way she’s treated, why everybody en masse doesn’t walk out. Nawaz: I take your point. I think I share your outrage and your concern. I will say, hats off to the women who are in that position every day, because I know the only thing they’re thinking about in that moment is the next question. That’s the only thing that they’re thinking about, because they’re pros and that’s what they show up to do. So I don’t even know that they want everybody to walk out. They would rather stay and continue to put tough questions to the president to be able to get answers on behalf of the people they serve. Again, it is a feature, not a bug. And that kind of misogyny, that kind of focus on women, we’ve seen much more aggressive stances against reporters of color, female reporters of color in particular, over the last 10-odd years. That is also a hallmark of autocratic and anti-democratic leaders around the world. There have been books and studies published on this, and it is becoming normalized, as you say. I mean, we can shout it out. We can point it out every time it happens. But because it happens so frequently and because there is so much else to cover, and there’s so many other headlines that those reporters themselves under attack want to focus on, we move on. Smith: I’m glad you brought up Marty Baron’s famous line from the first administration. Something to the effect of, as you said, Our job is not to go to war. Our job is to go to work. That’s the response to these threats from your perspective, whether it’s the chair of the FCC or the president’s social-media feed. We fight back with journalism, right? Goldberg: But going back to this point you’re making about who’s doing well and who’s not. The foundation of everything here is ownership that is stalwart. Marty Baron then was working for a Jeff Bezos who believed in the mission. That was then. Now is now. I mean, strangely, I’m the recipient of his moral incognizance, in the sense that we’ve hired close to 40 Washington Post journalists in the last year because they want to do journalism. But it all has to do with Sulzberger’s understanding the purpose of their institution and willing to stand up for the institution. And if you don’t mind me saying, it has to do with the owner of The Atlantic, who is stalwart about these things. And you’ve made the point. There’s a lot of different institutions on the TV side, on the print side, that simply don’t have strong, ethical, brave leaders. And I would say—I don’t even know if she’s here, but I’m saying it anyway. And I’m not blowing smoke at all; I really feel this—that we could not do anything if we didn’t know that the ownership had our back. I would only say, the perfect example of this is when I got involved in that Signal mess last year. Nawaz: Wait, what happened? Something happened? Smith: Everybody, cancel your 10 o’clock. Goldberg: When I got involved in that situation, it was very, very fraught. I mean, our lawyers had lawyers, and those lawyers had lawyers. Right now our general counsel is having a sharp intake of breath because he never knows what I’m going to say about this. But the very brief version of that—and I think Joe has probably experienced this and Amna has experienced this with PBS management—eventually the information reaches the ownership level. And Here’s the situation, and This is what we want to do. And the word from on high was, If it’s true and it’s important, publish it. And that sounds like basic journalism ownership. But not these days. And so that is the crucial difference. I’m very, very happy to have all these colleagues from my former newspaper The Washington Post join The Atlantic. I’m not happy about what happened at The Washington Post. We need strong newspapers in America, and you only have strong newspapers if you have strong leadership and ownership. Kahn: Right. Can I add one thing to that? Kahn: The Atlantic I know is celebrating its 169th year. The New York Times yesterday celebrated its 175th. So we’re ahead of you by a little bit. Goldberg: But we’re gonna catch up. We’re right on your tail. Kahn: We both have, in our mission statement, the word independence. Independent for 169 years, independent for 175 years. It’s another word that we use a lot, independent. And I’m not sure that everybody always appreciates what independence actually means. We know what it means to be independent of political partisan views. And we profess ourselves as journalists not to be partisans. And that’s true. But it also means independence of ownership, honestly. And the independence of ownership is increasingly important in this moment. And PBS experienced that because you receive money from the government and now you don’t. And that’s a pressure point. If you’re owned by a giant corporation that has a lot of other interests that are also regulated by the government, and they can manipulate or pull those levers, that is also not true independence. True independence is financial stability and independent ownership that does not have too many other interests that are at the discretion of the government to interfere with. And protecting independent journalism really does mean protecting those independent owners and supporting those independent owners. Nawaz: To that point, can I just say—to underscore your point about the fact that we as journalists can’t do our jobs without backers who want us to do the journalism, and underscoring your point that people want good journalism—the way that PBS viewers, viewers like you, stepped up. Goldberg: We’re one minute from handing out tote bags, by the way. Nawaz: I’m available outside, if you need help navigating to the website. But it does underscore both those points because people wanted to see good journalism. When our funding got cut, people did step up there. That is heartening. Goldberg: By the way, just to add, I’m sure most of you are New York Times subscribers, the day the Sulzbergers buy rocket ships is the day you should start worrying, right? Smith: Joe, you mentioned trust earlier, and you talked about how hard it is in an environment in which “the media” is not what we always thought of as “the media.” But I want to push on this a little bit because I do look at the Gallup surveys every year of the American public’s trust in media. Two years ago, the annual survey showed that 31 percent of the public had a “great deal” of trust or a “fair amount” of trust in the media. This was a historic low. This was in 2024. And I thought, Well, we’re in the basement. No place to go but up. They do the survey in 2025; turns out there’s another basement. Now we’re down to 28 percent. This is the new historic low in trust in the media. By the way, in case you’re wondering, the high was 72 percent, in 1976, 50 years ago. “No trust at all” in this last survey was 34 percent, a third of the country. And for those of us who live in red states, I do, that are run by Republican leaders—in case you’re wondering, they did it by party—Republicans as a party, 8 percent have a “great deal” of trust in the media. How did we get here? It’s not just the confusion between traditional journalism and people on TikTok. How did we get here? Jessica Yellin: The awkward adolescence of a media revolution Kahn: We live in a deeply polarized and partisan moment with a very confusing landscape that is called media. But there are some elements that are real and they’re challenges for us. You mentioned the Gallup survey. There’s also been a lot of Reuters work on this, and we’ve looked at those numbers really closely. And it’s very interesting that the further that news and information travels away from the individual human-being journalists who do the work, the less trust there is. So that most trust is still to the credible, hardworking, independent, real reporters on the beat, doing the job, knocking on doors, getting information and relaying that information, ideally in conversational terms, directly to the people who are consuming it. The more you move away from it toward platforms, toward social media, and at the low end of the spectrum, filtered through artificial intelligence, the less trust there is in the quality of that information. That’s all called media. But for us, what we’re trying to do to address this is to make more transparent the work that individual journalists are doing on the ground, putting their own expertise out there, making themselves vulnerable to get the information and then conveying that to the users of that as best as possible. Because it’s human beings; it’s a value that human beings bring to help explain and unearth new information that I think can help us. Smith: So that’s how we got here, and that’s also where we go from here. Goldberg: Don’t forget, there are active campaigns being run against the idea of independent journalism. Goldberg: This is what I meant before. Just because we’re not interested in that war doesn’t mean that war isn’t happening. And so people like Steve Bannon, and people on the left too, for that matter, it’s in their interest to undermine through lying the nature of what we do. And we again sit here and say the work speaks for itself, but the work doesn’t. But we also have to speak up. We have to almost lobby for ourselves and say, Look, there are people whose interest it is to undermine the idea that there’s some kind of fair truth out there. Smith: Let’s accept that there has been an intentional effort, call it the last 10 years, to undermine faith and confidence in a lot of institutions that hold people in power accountable. The media is just one. The plan is working as intended. But isn’t it also true that we own some of this as an industry? The call’s coming from inside the house? We’ve done some of this to ourselves. Nawaz: I think, to some of the points that have been made earlier, I think we’ve taken for granted the idea that people would just trust the information that we put out there because they always had. There was an idea that these are credible legacy institutions. Nawaz: To your point, back in 1976, the trust was high and we thought that would just continue. The 1976 media landscape was a universe away from where we are today. I mean, I wasn’t even born. Sorry, Jeff. Goldberg: I actually didn’t hear what she said. I don’t know what she’s apologizing for. I’ll just accept your apology. Nawaz: I will say, when you ask people about the media, I think there’s sort of a generalized—yes, there’s merchants of misinformation, people who attack the media as mistrustful and are agenda-driven. And so they automatically say, Yes, we don’t trust the media the same way we don’t trust Congress or we don’t trust the Supreme Court. All institutions are under that same skepticism. When you ask people individually, do you trust The New York Times? Do you trust The Atlantic? Smith: Well, PBS always indexes at the top of that list. Nawaz: We are second among most trusted brands right now, second only to the Weather Channel. Shoutout to the Weather Channel. And I think the same thing is true at the local level. To Joe’s point, people trust the people in their own community. They trust their local journalist, people they know, and we have to work harder on building those connections. Smith: So we have a minute left. Let’s solve the local journalism problem in one minute. Goldberg: But by the way, the solution to the national problem is at the local level. Which is to say that if there’s one problem that I would identify as our own fault, it’s hiring journalists who are trained at the same 50 colleges, and they have the same set of assumptions about the world. And, you know, The Harvard Crimson is great, but not everybody should come from the Harvard Crimson to our newsrooms. And there are a certain set of liberal assumptions that come in more than conservative assumptions. And to solve the problem, you recruit journalists and train journalists in places where people think slightly differently. It doesn’t mean that you can bring in people who can be fair and balanced, to borrow a slogan, into your newsrooms who just come from different backgrounds and have a different set of baseline assumptions. We’d be better off. Goldberg: Yeah, no, no, the working-class Daily Pennsylvanian. And you solve that problem—we’re really on the back foot on that—by having robust journalism presences in red states, in communities where we don’t draw journalists from: veterans, evangelical Christians, so on. And we have to do a better job of diversifying in all kinds of ways, the people who come in. Because they bring in their own experiences and they question implicit assumptions that people make. I’m not saying that there’s a liberal bias in the stories you read day to day. I think that there are—if you’re not careful about it, and I know that Joe tries to be careful about it, I try to be careful about it—just unspoken assumptions that people make about the way the world is organized. And that’s not great. That’s not the main problem. The main problem is that there’s a war against us, and that war is against the idea of truth. But these are things that we have to think about. Smith: So we end where we began. I would love to have more time to talk to you all, but we have a full day ahead of us. Please give a big hand to Amna Nawaz, Joe Kahn, and Jeffrey Goldberg.
